  • Машинное обучение: ты знаешь, что такое переобучение (overfitting), зачем нужна валидация на внешних данных и почему регуляризация — это «бритва Оккама» для модели.
  • Химия и структура: ты видишь связь «структура — функция» белка как продолжение знакомой рецепторной фармакологии: сродство, ключ и замок, точечная мутация.

💡 Суть

Критерий 80% простой и жёсткий: можешь объяснить понятие коллеге одним предложением без жаргона — и знаешь, где оно ломается. Если да — эта коробка закрыта. Если «вроде понимаю, но объяснить не могу» — она ещё открыта.

🔬 Глубже: почему именно объяснить, а не «прочитать»

Есть техника Фейнмана: по-настоящему ты понял тему только тогда, когда можешь растолковать её простыми словами тому, кто не в теме. Пока в объяснении есть дыры, которые ты прикрываешь умными словами, — там и сидит непонимание. Поэтому чеклист построен на «объясни и покажи, где ломается», а не на «отметь галочку, что прочитал». Пассивное узнавание обманчиво: текст кажется знакомым, но воспроизвести ты его не можешь. Активное припоминание — вот что оставляет след.

Как продолжать учиться

Книга закончилась — учёба нет. Но дальше всё меняется: теперь ты учишься не по главам, а по задачам. И это гораздо приятнее.

  • Практикуйся на своих данных. Не на учебном датасете про ирисы, а на том, что тебе действительно интересно: твоя специальность, твои пациенты (обезличенно!), твой клинический вопрос. Мотивация из любопытства держит в разы дольше, чем «надо».
  • Относись к темам как к ротациям. Как в ординатуре ты пробуешь кардиологию, потом неврологию и понимаешь, где твоё, — так попробуй месяц геномики, месяц медицинской визуализации, месяц ML. Специализацию выбирают руками, а не по описанию.
  • Учи то, что нужно прямо сейчас. Не «весь Python», а именно тот инструмент, который решает твою текущую задачу. Знание, применённое сегодня, остаётся. Знание «про запас» испаряется.
  • Возвращайся к основам по спирали. Через полгода перечитай главу про статистику — ты увидишь в ней вдвое больше, потому что теперь у тебя есть контекст. Это не топтание на месте, это повторение с новой глубины.

🩺 Мостик от медицины

Ты уже мастер обучения на реальных случаях. Ни один врач не выучился по учебнику — учишься у постели больного, на разборах, на своих ошибках. Data science устроен так же: настоящее умение приходит не из чтения, а из возни с настоящими, грязными, упрямыми данными. Один доведённый до конца свой проект стоит десяти пройденных курсов.

Ты закрыл книгу. Как эффективнее всего продолжить?

Знание закрепляется применением, а не накоплением. Один доведённый проект на своих данных даст больше, чем стопка непрочитанных до конца учебников — и вскроет именно те пробелы, которые стоит закрыть.

Единственное исключение: математику надо добить отдельно

Всю книгу я держал обещание: никаких выводов формул, никакого «реши интеграл». Мы брали интуицию, а не алгебру. И это было правильно — чтобы ты не утонул на первой странице.

Но будь честен с собой: это был обход, а не отмена. Математика — родной язык этой области. Пока ты читаешь на нём со словарём, ты зависишь от чужого перевода. Чтобы в какой-то момент перестать зависеть, её придётся добрать — отдельно и спокойно, уже без спешки этой книги.

  • Немного линейной алгебры — потому что данные живут в ней: таблица это матрица, PCA это её геометрия.
  • Основы вероятности — потому что вся статистика и всё ML стоят на ней, и без неё они остаются заклинаниями.
  • Идея производной — не техника счёта, а смысл: «как быстро одно меняется вслед за другим». Ты это уже знаешь под именем «период полувыведения».

🩺 Мостик от медицины

Дифференциальное уравнение звучит страшно, пока не назвать его по-твоему: фармакокинетика. Кривая концентрации препарата в плазме — это и есть решение уравнения «сколько вошло, сколько вышло». Ты интуитивно решаешь такие уравнения каждый раз, когда думаешь про нагрузочную и поддерживающую дозу. Математика не добавляет тебе новую способность — она даёт имя той, что уже есть.

💡 Суть

Математика — единственное, что эта книга сознательно отложила, а не отменила. Не бойся её и не боготвори. Добери её позже, маленькими порциями, привязывая каждое понятие к тому, что ты уже делаешь как врач.

Твоя слабость всё это время была силой

Ты, наверное, начинал эту книгу с тихой мыслью: «я же врач, не программист, мне будет тяжелее всех». Пора эту мысль перевернуть.

Инженер, пришедший в биомедицину, годами учится тому, что у тебя в костях: как устроена болезнь, что значит цифра для живого пациента, какой вопрос вообще стоит задавать. Он умеет строить модели — но часто строит их не про то. Ты знаешь, про что. И это невозможно нагуглить.

🎬 Аналогия

Представь двоих у одного микроскопа. Один блестяще настраивает оптику, но не знает, здоровая перед ним ткань или больная. Другой с первого взгляда видит патологию, но пока путается в винтах. Кому быстрее стать целым? Тому, кто уже видит патологию. Настроить оптику — вопрос практики. Видеть болезнь — это годы, которые у тебя уже есть.

💡 Суть

Data science даёт инструменты. Медицина даёт вопросы, которые стоят того, чтобы их задать, и чутьё, верен ли ответ. Инструменты доучиваются. Клиническое мышление — нет. Ты стоишь не в конце очереди, а в редкой её части: на пересечении, где людей мало, а нужны они позарез.

Так что 80% — это не финиш. Это порог, за которым ты уже полезен: можешь понять чужой анализ, поспорить по делу, сделать свой честный проект. Остальное дойдёшь по дороге — на своих данных, на своих вопросах, в своём темпе. Медленно — это нормально. Главное — не останавливаясь.

В чём главное преимущество врача, входящего в data science, перед инженером?

Инструменты и математика доучиваются практикой. А клиническое чутьё — какой вопрос осмыслен и правдоподобен ли результат — это годы у постели больного, которые нельзя нагуглить.

🎯 Что унести из главы

  • Дисциплины — это конвейер, не список. От клинического вопроса через данные, omics и снижение размерности к модели — и обратно к пациенту.
  • Есть правильный порядок. Мышление данными → Python → статистика → визуализация → своя область → ML в самом конце.
  • 80% = можешь объяснить и применить. Порог не для галочки, а для реальной работы и разговора на равных.
  • Учись дальше на своих данных. Один доведённый проект и «ротации» по темам обгоняют стопку курсов.
  • Математику добери отдельно. Единственное, что книга отложила, а не отменила — маленькими порциями, через знакомую физиологию.
  • Медицина — твой козырь. Ты знаешь, какой вопрос стоит задать. Это редко и этому не учат на курсах.
🧭

Prologue

Why this book, and how to use it

⏱ ~8 min · After this chapter you'll know what the program has in store for you, and how to read this book so the knowledge actually sticks.

You're a doctor. You can read a person like an open book — from the shortness of breath, the color of the sclerae, the way a patient lowers themselves onto the exam table. And now someone tells you: "go learn the language of matrices, probabilities, and algorithms." It sounds like moving to a foreign country without a dictionary. This book is your dictionary. And here's a small spoiler: you already speak half of this language — it just goes by different names.

How biology became quantitative

Just forty years ago, biology was a science of observation. You looked down a microscope, described what you saw, and drew little arrows between molecules. Then a few quiet revolutions happened.

First we learned to read the genome — and instead of one gene per paper, a single experiment suddenly yielded twenty thousand genes at once. Then came instruments that measure thousands of proteins, metabolites, and individual cells in a single run. And all at once the bottleneck was no longer the experiment, but the question: what on earth do we do with all of this?

When data is scarce, the eye and intuition are enough. When you have twenty thousand columns and a million rows, the eye goes blind. You need a different instrument — mathematics and algorithms. Not because it's fashionable, but because otherwise the signal drowns in the noise. That's how biology became quantitative: it didn't change its subject, it changed the instrument it sees with.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You've already lived through this shift in miniature. Diagnoses used to be made by auscultation and palpation. Then came the ECG, the CT scanner, the lab panels — and the physician started reading numbers and graphs, not only the body. Quantitative biology is the very same thing, but for the cell and the genome: the instruments hand you numbers, and you learn to read them. You're not abandoning the clinic. You're just adding one more instrument — a computational one.

What this program is, and why you need this book

You're heading into the master's program in Quantitative Life Science at Constructor University (Bremen). The name is honest: "quantitative life sciences." It's a crossroads where biology, statistics, programming, and the physics of systems all meet. In programs like this, people don't stumble over biology — you know that better than many of your professors. They stumble over the language: over probability, models, algorithms, over words like "regularization" or "clustering" that sound terrifying right up until someone explains them in plain human terms.

💡 The gist

This book gives you roughly 80% of the conceptual foundation of the whole program — the intuition behind every important idea. There's one thing it deliberately does not do: teach you pure mathematics (deriving formulas, proofs, "now solve this integral"). The math will be given to you separately and in full. Our job is that by the time you open that course, you already understand what it's even about. Meaning first, formulas later.

Think of the program as an opera in an unfamiliar language. You can show up and spend an hour and a half guessing what they're singing about. Or you can read the libretto beforehand — and then every aria suddenly makes sense. This book is your libretto.

What does this book deliberately NOT do?

The book gives you conceptual intuition (including the biological meaning and the clinical bridges), while the formal mathematics is something you study in a separate course. Here it's meaning, not calculation.

The good news: you already speak half of this language

The nicest discovery is still ahead of you: almost every "scary" quantitative concept is a renamed version of something you use at the bedside every single day.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

Bayes' theorem sounds like higher mathematics. In practice it's exactly what you do when a positive troponin in a 25-year-old with reflux means something entirely different from the same result in a 70-year-old with crushing substernal chest pain. The pre-test probability is your prior knowledge, the test result is the update, and the post-test probability is the answer. Bayes is just the arithmetic of the sentence "I wasn't worried until that result came back — and now I am."

🩺 Bridge from medicine

Overfitting is another old acquaintance. Every doctor has seen a risk score that dazzled in the original paper and flopped in their own hospital. That's overfitting: the model memorized the quirks of one cohort instead of the disease itself. And your reflex of "I won't trust a rule until it's been checked on patients like mine" — that is exactly external validation in the language of the program.

There are dozens of these bridges in the book. Pharmacokinetics is differential equations: a drug's concentration falls by the same laws that lie behind the idea of a "half-life." The epidemic curve from the news is a model of how an infection spreads. PCA is what you do when you collapse forty measurements into a single syndrome like "looks septic." You're not learning a language from scratch. You're extending a bridge from the bank you already stand on.

🎬 Analogy

Remember your first shift in the ICU? At first the monitors are a wall of blinking numbers and beeping — pure chaos. A month later, a single glance tells you the saturation is drifting down and the rhythm has gone wrong — before the alarm even fires. Quantitative biology, right now, is that wall of monitors on your first day. This book is that month of shifts, compressed into a few evenings. The goal isn't for you to memorize the numbers, but for the wall to suddenly become readable.

How the book is built

Every chapter is assembled from the same parts — so you don't spend energy figuring out "how does this thing work" and can pour it into the material itself. Here's your toolkit:

  • 🩺 Bridges from medicine — the signature feature. In every chapter, a new concept lands on something you already know as a doctor. This isn't decoration, it's the load-bearing structure of the whole book.
  • 💡 The gist — if you carry away one thought from the entire chapter, let it be this one. Come back to these when you're in a hurry.
  • 🎬 Analogies and stories — because the brain remembers a vivid scene and forgets a dry definition within a week.
  • 🔬 Deeper — collapsed asides for the curious. Want to dig in? Expand it. In a hurry? Walk past guilt-free — it won't break the main thread.
  • Terms with tooltips — hover your cursor and a definition appears. No trips to the dictionary.
  • Flashcards and mini-quizzes — not for testing you, but for training your memory. More on these separately, because they matter most of all.
🔬 Deeper: why the chapters are built "in a spiral"

You'll notice that some ideas come back across different chapters — first in passing, then more deeply. That's not the author being forgetful, it's by design. A concept you've met three times in increasing complexity settles far more firmly than one dumped on you whole in a single go. One reader will glide along the main thread, another will dive into the details on the same page — and both get what they came for. It's called spiral delivery, and the brain loves it.

How to read so the knowledge stays

Now for the most important part — and it's not about content, it's about method. There's a way of reading in which what you've read evaporates by morning. And there's a way in which it stays for years. The difference is enormous, and it costs almost nothing.

💡 The gist

Rereading is almost useless. The brain remembers not what it took in, but what it recalled with effort. This is called active recall, and it's the most well-proven technique in the science of learning.

The difference here is almost indecent. In the classic experiment by Roediger and Karpicke (2006), those who recalled the material after reading retained about 60% a week later. Those who simply reread — around 40%. The same time spent, and noticeably more sticks. That's why quizzes and flashcards are scattered throughout the book: they're not an exam, they're a gym. Every time you honestly try to answer before you peek, you're literally burning the memory in.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You've already proven this on yourself. Remember cramming for your board exams: you could reread the textbook for the tenth time and remember nothing, or you could quiz each other — and that's when it all clicked into place. Or simpler: you remember anatomy not because you read the atlas, but because they drilled you on the specimens, and you had to pull the answer out of your head under the professor's gaze. That's active recall. It works because the effort to remember is the memory training.

The second technique is spaced repetition. Don't cram a chapter to death in one sitting. It's far better to come back to the flashcards tomorrow, then in three days, then in a week. Each time you've almost forgotten and yet manage to recall, the trace in your memory grows deeper. That's exactly why the flashcards at the ends of chapters repeat and overlap: the book deliberately makes you meet old ideas in new places.

🎬 Analogy

Memory is like a path through a forest. Walk it once, and by the next day the grass has straightened back up — no trace left. But if you keep returning to the same path at intervals — today, the day after tomorrow, a week from now — it gets trodden into a road that can no longer be overgrown. Cramming the night before an exam is treading the path a hundred times in one hour: by morning it's grown over anyway. Spaced repetition is treading it ten times, but over ten days. Less effort, and the road stays forever.

And the third, almost imperceptible one: before you read the explanation, try to guess the answer yourself. Even a wrong guess isn't wasted — it prepares a "shelf" in your head where the right answer will settle more firmly. That's why many chapters pose a question first and only then answer it. Don't skip this moment: a second of your own guess is worth a paragraph of passive reading.

You've read a chapter and want to remember it for the long haul. What works best?

Active recall with intervals clearly beats rereading for retention a week out. The effort to recall is the memory training; highlighting and rereading create only the illusion of knowing.

A word about pace, and about how it's normal not to get it right away

There will be paragraphs that don't land the first time. That's not a bug, it's a feature. There's a lovely idea in the science of learning: the techniques that make learning harder in the moment — recalling instead of peeking, returning instead of cramming in one burst — are the ones that give the most durable result. They're actually called desirable difficulties. If it feels tough in places, that means it's working. Easy reading that leaves nothing behind — that's what to be afraid of.

And one more thing: don't try to swallow the book in a single go. One or two chapters an evening, with the quizzes and flashcards, and your brain has time to file the material away. Progress accumulates on its own, chapter by chapter.

💡 The gist

Read it not like a novel — like a game you're playing. Guess → check → recall later. An active reader remembers many times more than a spectator. This book is designed as a gym, not a lecture — so use it that way.

Flashcards: try them right now

Here's your first deck. Don't peek right away — read the question, try to answer it in your head, and only then flip the card. That's active recall in action.

You've hit a paragraph that was tough going and didn't click the first time. What does that mean?

"Desirable difficulties" are techniques that feel harder in the moment but cement the material most firmly. Easy reading that leaves nothing behind is the real problem — not manageable effort.

So there you have it: the dictionary is in your hands, the method is clear, and the bridge from your bank is already in sight. Ahead lies the language itself — the one the whole program speaks. I promise: by the end of the book, the wall of monitors will become readable. Let's go.

🎯 Key takeaways

  • Biology didn't become quantitative out of fashion: instruments buried us in data, and without mathematics the eye goes blind in it.
  • This book gives you ~80% of the conceptual foundation of the Quantitative Life Science program — the intuition behind the concepts, but NOT pure mathematics (that's taught separately).
  • Most "scary" quantitative ideas are renamed clinical reasoning you already command. That's where the bridges from medicine come from.
  • Read actively: first guess, then check, then recall later. Active recall clearly beats rereading for retention.
  • Come back to the flashcards at growing intervals — spaced repetition treads the path in your memory into a road.
  • If it's tough in places, that's it working. Desirable difficulties produce the most durable knowledge. One or two chapters an evening, no rushing.
🔭

Chapter 1

The big picture: how biology became a data science

⏱ ~14 min · After this chapter you'll see the whole book as a single map, and you'll know exactly where your clinical intuition becomes an advantage rather than a liability.

Twenty years ago a biologist peered down a microscope and said, "the cells look sick." Today the instrument spits out a table twenty thousand rows long — one row per gene — and the question sounds different: "which of these numbers matter, and how do we know we haven't fooled ourselves?" This isn't a cosmetic change. It's a change in the organ of sight itself.

What exactly is quantitative life science

Quantitative life science (the quantitative sciences of life) isn't a separate discipline like cardiology. It's more of a way of looking. A way in which any statement about the living — "this protein is more active," "this tumour is more aggressive," "this patient will respond to treatment" — is translated into a measurable quantity, and then stress-tested with the tools of mathematics and code.

Biology used to be a qualitative science: more or less, present or absent, "the cell is activated." Now it's quantitative: how many times more, and with what probability the difference is real rather than random ripple. And this is exactly where the physician suddenly feels at home — because reading lab results is precisely how you've thought your whole career.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You've never said "the patient's potassium is high" without a number. You say "6.2 against an upper limit of 5.0" — and you instantly feel how far out of range that is. That's quantitative thinking in its purest form: value, reference, deviation. Quantitative life science does the very same thing, only across the whole genome at once instead of a single line on a metabolic panel.

Why everything changed in 20 years: the big instruments arrived

The turning point came not from a new idea but from new machines. Three of them rewrote the rules.

Sequencing. Reading the first human genome (a project completed in 2003) cost roughly three billion dollars and took more than ten years. Today it costs less than your smartphone and is done in a day. The instrument no longer answers the question "which letter is here?" — it dumps out billions of letters, and now it's your job to find the meaning in them.

Mass spectrometry. To measure a single protein, you once had to set up a week-long experiment. A modern mass spectrometer weighs thousands of proteins and metabolites in a drop of blood in a single run. Picture a blood panel not of 15 markers, but of 5000 — all at once.

High-content microscopy. An automated microscope photographs not the one field of view you study with your eyes, but hundreds of thousands of cells overnight, measuring the shape, brightness and organelle positions of each. One experiment yields terabytes of images that no human could ever review by hand in a whole lifetime.

💡 The gist

Biology became quantitative not because biologists got smarter, but because the instruments learned to measure everything, all at once. Data came flooding in — and the old tool for making sense of it, "look with your eyes," simply couldn't keep up with the volume.

And here's what's worth feeling: when data is scarce, the eye and experience are the best analyser. When it runs to terabytes, the eye goes blind. You need a new way to see.

🎬 Analogy

Leeuwenhoek's microscope first showed people bacteria — a whole invisible world opened up, because a new lens had appeared. Data today is exactly that kind of lens. A sequencer "sees" a single-letter difference in the DNA between a healthy cell and a cancerous one that no eye could ever make out. Data is the microscope of the 21st century. Only you'll bring it into focus not with a knob, but with code and statistics.

The loop that all of data science rests on

How does knowledge arise from a stream of numbers? Through a simple, almost clinical four-step loop that you'll meet again and again in this book:

  • Measure. The instrument gives you raw data — sequences, spectra, images.
  • Model. You build a simplified picture: what pattern could have produced these numbers?
  • Predict. The model makes a testable claim about something you haven't seen yet.
  • Test. You go to a new experiment or to new patients — and see whether it held up. Then the loop starts over, wiser this time.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

This loop is your everyday clinical cycle. You measure (gather complaints, labs, imaging), model (build a differential diagnosis — a simplified hypothesis about what's going on), predict ("if this is pneumonia, the antibiotic will break the fever within 48 hours") and test (look at the patient two days later). Data science didn't invent anything new — it simply formalised a physician's reasoning and taught the machine to spin that loop across millions of cases at once.

What exactly did the arrival of sequencing, mass spectrometry and high-content microscopy change?

It's not about researchers being cleverer, nor about scrapping instruments, but about scale: when an instrument produces terabytes, hand analysis is physically impossible — and that forced biology to become quantitative.

Wet lab and dry lab: two hands of one body

A division of labour has grown up in the modern laboratory. The wet lab is test tubes, cells, reagents, pipettes: the place where data is born. The dry lab is computers, code, models: the place where data is turned into meaning.

It's tempting to think of these as two different tribes. In truth they're two hands of one body, and the best science happens when they're clasped together. A wet experiment without analysis produces data that will lie dead weight on a disk. Analysis without a wet experiment builds beautiful castles on sand, with no one to check them.

🎬 Analogy

The wet lab is the operating theatre: there you cut, observe, wrest a fact out with your hands. The dry lab is the doctors' room, where people think, argue and decide over the scans and labs. A great surgeon who never once sat down to work through a result, and a brilliant diagnostician who never once saw a patient in the flesh, both lose — alone — to the team where those two talk to each other every day.

🔬 Deeper: why a physician should at least peek into the dry lab

You don't have to become a programmer. But even a basic grasp of the dry lab changes how you read papers. When a study says "the model predicted treatment response with 92% accuracy," a physician with quantitative literacy immediately asks: accuracy on the same patients the model learned from, or on new ones? How many patients were there? And what about the ones the model wrongly filed under "won't respond"? These questions separate the paper that will change your practice from the paper that looks lovely and falls apart in a real clinic. This whole book, in essence, teaches you to ask questions like that.

The physician's place in this world — and why it's the best seat in the house

It's easy to get scared here: "I'm not a mathematician, I'll be pushed out by people with laptops." It's exactly the opposite. In a world where anyone can train a model in a couple of lines of code, the scarce resource is no longer code. What's scarce is the person who understands which question is even worth asking, and which answer is clinically plausible.

A model will happily find a "pattern" between the colour of the doctor's coat and survival, if you ask it to. It doesn't know that's nonsense. You do. Clinical intuition is a built-in nonsense detector, tuned by thousands of living patients, and it can't be downloaded.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You're already a master of working with noisy, incomplete, contradictory data — that's every patient. When a result "doesn't fit" the clinical picture, you double-check it rather than swallow it blindly. That very habit — telling signal from noise — sits at the heart of all quantitative science. A data scientist spends years learning it. You already have it, polished at the bedside.

💡 The gist

A model is a differential diagnosis expressed in the language of mathematics. The features are symptoms and labs, the outcome probabilities are a ranked list of hypotheses, and the model tunes its weights not from a textbook but from data. Once you see this, almost every method in this book stops feeling foreign.

Why is a physician's clinical intuition an advantage in a world of data, rather than a relic?

A model will find a correlation in anything, but doesn't know what's meaningful. The physician brings knowledge of plausibility — a rare resource that can't be automated. You still need to check models (option 2 is wrong).

The overview map: how everything in this book connects

Ahead of you are chapters on statistics, code, machine learning, omics, modelling, chemistry and biophysics. From the outside — seven different subjects. In reality — seven tools around one loop, "measure → model → predict → test." Here's how they add up to a single whole.

  • Statistics is the discipline of honesty. It answers the central clinical question: is this difference real, or did I just imagine it? It's the language in which data confesses how far it can be trusted.
  • Programming is your hands in the dry lab. You can't sift terabytes by hand; code is the way to hand the drudgery to the machine and keep the thinking for yourself.
  • Machine learning is the art of finding patterns too complex to write down as a formula. That very "differential diagnosis trained on data."
  • Omics — genomics, proteomics, metabolomics: measuring the living in whole layers at once. These are the big instruments from the start of the chapter, and the avalanche of data that everything else teaches you to tame.
  • Modelling — building working "mini-versions" of living systems: how an epidemic spreads, how a drug distributes through the body. Your pharmacokinetics is already this.
  • Chemistry and biophysics — the foundation under it all: why a protein folds just so, why a molecule binds a receptor. Your receptor pharmacology, gone quantitative.

🎬 Analogy

Think of this book as a set of tools in the hands of a detective. Omics brings in a mountain of evidence. Code files it onto shelves. Statistics sifts the chance coincidences from the real leads. Machine learning lines the suspects up by probability. Modelling and biophysics explain the mechanism of the crime. And you, the physician, are the detective who in the final scene says which theory actually makes sense. Tools without the detective are useless.

🔬 Deeper: why we won't be doing the maths

In this book you won't meet a single equation you need to "solve." And that's a deliberate choice, not a dumbing-down for the weak. The reason is simple: to use a tool wisely, you don't have to be able to build it from scratch. You prescribe warfarin without deriving its binding constant by hand; you read an ECG without solving the Nernst equation on paper — yet you understand that ion movement across a membrane lies behind it. In just the same way, we'll take each concept for its meaning and intuition, not its formula. Understanding what a method does and when it lies is worth more than being able to grind through its arithmetic.

What unites statistics, code, ML, omics and modelling into one book?

Each method serves some step of the single loop: omics measures, ML models, statistics checks the honesty. That's precisely why they form a whole rather than a random anthology — and no advanced mathematics is needed to understand them.

Bringing it into one view

Here are five cards worth committing to memory before we dive into the details. Click to test yourself.

🎯 Key takeaways

  • Biology became quantitative not from new ideas but from new instruments: sequencers, mass spectrometers and automated microscopes dumped out terabytes, and "looking with your eyes" stopped working.
  • All of data science runs on one loop — measure, model, predict, test — and that's exactly your clinical cycle, only wound up to run on millions of cases.
  • Wet lab and dry lab aren't rivals but two hands: one wrests out the data, the other extracts the meaning; the power is born in their conversation.
  • Data is the microscope of our age, and code and statistics are how you bring it into focus.
  • In a world where a model learns in a couple of lines, what's scarce isn't code but the person who knows which question is meaningful and which answer is plausible — that is, the physician.
  • The seven disciplines of this book aren't disconnected subjects but tools around one loop; ahead, we'll take each one for its meaning, not its formula.
📐

Chapter 2

Thinking in data: from raw signal to matrix

⏱ ~16 min · After this chapter you'll see any biological experiment as a table — and understand where its truth comes from, and where its noise sneaks in

A sequencer doesn't "read DNA." It measures flashes of light. A blood-pressure cuff doesn't "know" your pressure — it senses vibrations inside the sleeve. Between reality and a number there is always a chain of transformations. And almost everything you'll learn in the rest of this book begins with one modest discovery: however different they may seem, all biological data ultimately settle into the very same rectangular table.

The shift this whole chapter is built around

A doctor thinks in patients. One person, one story: complaints, labs, how things are trending. That's exactly right at the bedside. But the moment you sit down with data from a thousand patients, you need a different angle — not "this story," but "this table." In your mind you stop reading the rows one at a time and start seeing the columns: how a feature behaves across all the people at once.

That turn of the head — from "patient" to "variable" — is the core skill of quantitative biology. It doesn't cancel clinical thinking; it widens it. So let's walk the whole path: from a flash of light to a row in a table you can actually analyze.

💡 The gist

Data isn't reality itself — it's an imprint of reality, taken by an instrument. The imprint is what you'll actually work with. So understanding how it was taken, and where it's distorted, isn't fussiness — it's professional hygiene.

What a measurement is — and what it actually gives you

To measure is to assign a number to a slice of reality according to a rule agreed on in advance. The key words here are "rule" and "in advance." A thermometer reads 37.2 because someone once agreed how to turn the expansion of mercury into degrees. Strip away the agreement and the number becomes a meaningless tick mark.

From this follows something important: a measurement never hands you "truth directly." It hands you an estimate of the truth, taken through an instrument — with its sensitivity, its range, and its quirks. A number is always a little bit about the device, not only about the object. A good researcher keeps this in mind at all times.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already think this way about the lab. A CRP of "5 mg/L" isn't some Platonic essence of inflammation — it's the output of one particular analyzer with one particular calibration. It doesn't surprise you that two labs have slightly different reference ranges, does it? That's exactly the healthy attitude toward measurement: the number is a reading from a device, not the disease itself.

Signal → number → table: three transformations

Let's trace the chain on a live example — RNA-seq (measuring gene activity).

First, the signal. Inside the instrument a physical process is unfolding: fluorescent flashes, an electric current through a nanopore, the absorption of light. The raw signal is an analog wave — the "raw material," which doesn't yet contain a single gene.

Then, the number. A processing program recognizes the letters in the signal, assembles them into short fragments, aligns them against a reference genome, and counts: how many times did the gene TP53 show up in this sample? Say, 1,240 times. And there it is — a number, born from a wave of light.

Finally, the table. Repeat this for 20,000 genes and for 50 patients, and you have a rectangle of numbers. Each gene is a row, each patient a column, and at the intersection sits how active that gene is in that person. The chaos of flashes has become a grid you can sort, compare, and plot.

🎬 Analogy

Picture an orchestra. In the hall there's a solid wall of sound (the signal). The sound engineer splits it into tracks: here the violins, there the horns, each with its own loudness over time (the numbers). And the score on the stand is already a table: instruments down the rows, bars across the columns. A score you can read, transpose, and search for whoever hit a wrong note. A living wall of sound — you can't. Analysis always works with the score, never with the concert.

The universal shape: the "features × samples" matrix

And now — the thing that made the walk worthwhile. Almost any biological data, however exotic the method sounds, folds into a single shape: the "features × samples" matrix.

  • Samples are the "units of observation": patients, cells, test tubes, time points. Usually they run down the columns.
  • Features are "what we measured about each one": genes, proteins, metabolites, blood markers. Usually they run down the rows.
  • At the intersection sits one measured number: the value of that feature in that sample.

Gene expression? Genes × patients. Proteomics? Proteins × samples. Flow cytometry? Markers × cells. Even a medical chart, once you tidy it up, is features (age, blood pressure, diagnosis) × patients. Once you learn to see this grid, you'll recognize it everywhere. Hence the motto of this chapter: almost everything becomes a table.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You've already held a matrix like this in your hands — it's the ward round sheet in the hospital. Down the left, a column of parameters (Hb, white cells, creatinine, temperature); across the top, the beds or the days. The clinician's eye runs along the "creatinine" row and catches whose number has started creeping up. That's exactly how a bioinformatician's mind moves along a row of the matrix. The only difference is scale: not 8 parameters, but 20,000.

A gene-expression matrix has 20,000 gene-rows and 60 patient-columns. What does a single number at an intersection mean?

A cell of a "features × samples" matrix is always a single measurement: the value of one feature (a gene) in one sample (a patient). There's no averaging and no diagnosis inside it.

Not all numbers are equal: types of data

Before you compute anything, ask yourself: what kind of number is this, exactly? Mixing types is a classic beginner's mistake — one that makes an analysis quietly lie to you. Three main kinds:

  • Numerical — genuine quantities that arithmetic is allowed to touch: potassium concentration, height, a gene's read count. Averages and differences are meaningful here.
  • Categorical — labels with no order: blood type, sex, mutation "present/absent." The "average" of blood group A and blood group B is nonsense, like the average of an apple and a Tuesday.
  • Ordinal — labels with an order but no honest scale of distances: cancer stage I–IV, a pain scale of 0–10, severity "mild / moderate / severe." The order is real, but "III minus I = II" can no longer be claimed: the gaps between stages aren't uniform.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

Ordinal data is your native tongue. The Apgar score, the Glasgow Coma Scale, the mMRC dyspnea grade — these are ranks, not a ruler. You wouldn't say a patient with a Glasgow of 8 is "exactly twice as conscious" as one with 4, would you? So an algorithm mustn't treat these numbers as grams either. Keeping data types apart is the same reflex as refusing to fold cancer stages into an arithmetic mean.

The stage of chronic kidney disease (CKD) is coded as 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. What type of data is this?

The stages are lined up by severity (the order is real), but the "distance" from stage 1 to 2 isn't equal to the distance from 4 to 5. Computing an average stage as if it were an ordinary number is a trap.

Noise, error, and the difference between "precise" and "correct"

No measurement is ever perfect. Two troubles are woven into every number, and it pays to tell them apart.

Noise (random error) is a tremor. Measure the same thing ten times and you'll get ten slightly different numbers, scattered around a center. Noise is unpredictable in the small, but it averages out: the more repeats, the calmer the result.

Systematic error (bias) is a shift. The instrument lies steadily in one direction: the scale wasn't zeroed, the reagent has expired. However many times you repeat, the center stays displaced. Repeats don't rescue you here, because they're all crooked in the same way.

Hence two different words that everyday speech tends to blur:

  • Accuracy — how close the average of your measurements is to the true value. This is about the shift.
  • Precision (reproducibility) — how tightly the repeats cluster together, regardless of where that cluster sits. This is about the tremor.

🎬 Analogy

A target. Accuracy is whether you hit the center. Precision is how tightly the holes group. A tight cluster in the corner of the target — high precision, no accuracy at all: the shooter is steady, but the sights are off. Holes scattered around the bull's-eye — the opposite: correct on average, but unreliable. The worst case is tight and off: you're confident in the answer, and it's biased. Systematic error is the most dangerous of all, precisely because it looks like a reliable result.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

This is exactly the logic of lab quality control. A poorly calibrated glucometer can spit out eight nearly identical values in a row (high precision) — all eight sitting 2 mmol/L below the truth (zero accuracy). A steady answer ≠ a correct answer. The clinician who re-checks a suspiciously smooth result on a different device is intuitively hunting for exactly this systematic shift.

🔬 Deeper: why systematic error is so hard to catch

Random noise gives itself away by its scatter — you see the tremor and grow wary. A systematic shift stays silent: all the repeats agree with one another, and the picture looks convincing. You can only catch it with an external anchor — a standard, a control sample, a second independent method. That's why decent experiments always have "controls" running alongside: it's not bureaucracy, it's the only way to notice that your whole beautiful cluster is lying in the wrong place. The same principle governs the external validation of clinical models: until a model is checked on someone else's cohort, you don't know whether it lands tightly off-target or right on it.

Normalization: why you tidy numbers before comparing them

Here's a scene that wrecks a naive analysis. You sequenced two samples. In the first, the instrument counted 40 million reads that day; in the second, 20 million. The gene ACTB: 1,000 reads in the first, 800 in the second. Where is it more active?

The temptation is to say "in the first — there are more, after all." But the second sample also has half as many reads overall — the instrument simply got less material. Let's convert to a common yardstick: reads per million. In the first: 1,000 out of 40 million is 25 per million. In the second: 800 out of 20 million is 40 per million. So in fact the gene is denser in the second sample, even though the raw number there is smaller. Without the correction you'd have been comparing not biology but the sequencing depth of that particular day — and you'd have reached exactly the opposite conclusion.

Normalization is precisely this: bringing numbers to a common yardstick so they become comparable. Strip out the technical differences — volume, instrument sensitivity, reagent batch — and leave only the biology. Without it, you're confidently comparing oranges to batteries.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You normalize every day without calling it that. Creatinine clearance isn't reported in absolute terms but adjusted for body surface area — otherwise you couldn't compare a child with an adult. A drug dose is recalculated per body weight or per GFR. Body mass index divides weight by height squared — that's literally normalizing weight "for the size of the person." Every time you divide by "something, so it becomes comparable," you're doing exactly what a bioinformatician does with reads.

💡 The gist

Before comparing two numbers, ask: are they even in the same coordinate system? Normalization is the leveling of starting conditions, so that the difference you see is real and not the shadow of a technical difference in volume or calibration.

The pipeline: raw → clean → matrix → analysis

Everything above adds up to an assembly line — a pipeline. Four stations:

  • Raw — whatever the instrument spat out: signal files, unprocessed reads. Huge, unwieldy, full of technical junk. Nobody analyzes anything here.
  • Clean — you cut out the defects: trim the low-quality ends of reads, throw away samples that are clearly ruined, patch the gaps. A dull station, but a decisive one.
  • Matrix — you assemble that very "features × samples" grid and normalize it. This is exactly where data becomes fit to analyze.
  • Analysis — only now: comparisons, hunting for patterns, plots, conclusions.

The order isn't decorative. You can't analyze raw material — there are no numbers there yet. Cleaning after analysis is too late — you've already drawn conclusions from junk. The line flows one way, and each station prepares the input for the next.

🎬 Analogy

This is histological processing. A piece of tissue (raw) can't go straight under the microscope. It's fixed, dehydrated, embedded in paraffin, cut on a microtome, stained (clean → matrix) — and only then placed on the stage and given a diagnosis (analysis). Try skipping fixation and cutting right away and you'll get mush. A data pipeline is the same processing, just for numbers: each step makes the material fit for the next.

At which station of the pipeline does normalization happen?

Normalization brings numbers to a common yardstick once the matrix is already assembled but the analysis hasn't begun. There's nothing to normalize earlier (no numbers), and later is too late (the conclusions are already skewed).

Why reproducibility begins right here

There's a quiet but grown-up reason to love pipelines. If every step — from raw material to plot — is recorded as a clear sequence, then a colleague (or you, a year from now) can run the same data through and get the same result. That's what reproducibility is — the bedrock of scientific trust.

Now recall the typical "hands in the spreadsheet" work: tweak a cell here, delete a row there, eyeball an outlier and toss it out. A month later nobody — the author included — can reproduce that chain. The result turns into a magic trick that worked exactly once. Reproducibility doesn't collapse at the stage of grand conclusions; it collapses quietly, at the very beginning, in the unrecorded cleaning of data.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

This is your principle from clinical trials, simply carried over to data. The protocol is written before the study and followed to the letter — so the result can't be dismissed as a matter of criteria being nudged along the way. A recorded pipeline is that same protocol, only for processing numbers. "So how exactly did you clean the data?" is just as legitimate a question as "what were the inclusion criteria?"

🎯 Key takeaways

  • Data is an imprint of reality, taken by an instrument. You always work with the imprint, so knowing its distortions is mandatory.
  • Every experiment travels the path signal → number → table; the output is almost always one shape — the "features × samples" matrix.
  • Numbers come in three kinds — numerical, categorical, ordinal — and must be handled differently; mixing types quietly breaks an analysis.
  • Distinguish precision (tight clustering) from accuracy (hitting the center). A systematic shift is more dangerous than noise: it looks reliable and can't be cured by repeats.
  • Normalization levels the starting conditions so you compare biology, not the instrument's calibration — like BMI or creatinine clearance.
  • Reproducibility is born not in the conclusions but in the recorded pipeline raw → clean → matrix → analysis. It's your study protocol, only for numbers.
📊

Chapter 3

Statistics as intuition (no formulas)

⏱ ~22 min · After this chapter you'll have a feel for when a result deserves your trust and when a number is just dancing on its own — and why p-value keeps luring you into a trap.

Picture this: a colleague bursts in waving a printout. "Look! Gene X differs between the sick and the healthy, p = 0.03!" You're almost delighted. But hold on — how many genes did they test in total? If it was twenty thousand, that delight is worth exactly as much as one "slightly elevated" value in a panel of forty tests. This chapter is about learning to smell traps like this a mile off — without a single formula.

Statistics isn't a set of tests to memorize. It's a way of thinking about uncertainty. And uncertainty is your native turf. Every day, at the bedside, you weigh "how sure am I" against "what if I'm wrong." We're simply going to give that weighing some names and learn not to fool ourselves.

💡 The gist

All of statistics answers a single question — the one you ask yourself a hundred times a day: is this a real finding, or did I just get lucky (or unlucky) with this particular patient? Signal versus noise. Everything else is just tooling for that one question.

Variation: why nothing ever repeats twice

Measure hemoglobin in ten healthy men. You won't get ten identical numbers. One is 145, another 158, another 139. That's not a lab error — it's variation, and it's everywhere: between people, between the cells of one person, between two tubes drawn from the same blood, between two runs of the same instrument.

A beginner sees two numbers — 145 and 158 — and thinks "they're different." Someone who thinks statistically asks it differently: "are they different by more than the spread I already expect anyway?" That's the whole of it. There's always a difference. The question is whether it exceeds the usual noise.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already carry reference ranges in your head. A sodium of 138 doesn't worry you; 128 worries you a lot. Why? Because you intuitively hold in mind how widely a value wanders in healthy people, and you only react when it steps outside that spread. A reference range is variation, tamed. Statistics just applies the same logic to everything under the sun.

Distributions are "shapes," not scary curves

Gather a lot of values and look at how they've landed, and you get a distribution — the shape of the data cloud. And, honestly, you only need a feel for three shapes.

  • Normal (the "bell"). Most values huddle near the middle; extremes are rare and symmetric. Height, hemoglobin, blood pressure across a population. When something is the sum of many small, independent causes, it likes to take this shape.
  • Poisson (counters of rare events). This is about "how many times something happened": the number of mutations per stretch of DNA, the number of ambulance calls in a night. The shape is skewed: zero and one are common, big numbers are rare. The key intuition here is that the spread grows along with the mean. Rare events don't get tidier. A caveat: at high depth (when the counts are large on average), even Poisson data stretch out into a long right tail and start to look, on the surface, log-normal.
  • Log-normal (the right tail). Most values are modest, but there's a long tail of very large ones. Gene expression levels, concentrations, antibody titers, incomes. No symmetry — a handful of giants drags the mean upward. That's why biology "takes the log" so often: the tail straightens back into the familiar bell.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already know log-normal in your gut. You record antibody titers as 1:160, 1:320, 1:640 — in doublings, not "+10 each time." And most people's "normal" CRP is low, but in a handful it goes off the scale. That's the right tail. When data "live by multiplication, not by addition," they're log-normal, and the arithmetic mean describes them poorly — the median is more honest.

You're handed read counts per gene from RNA-seq: most genes have very few, a handful have thousands. Which shape is closest?

Data like this are skewed right: many small values and a long tail of a few large ones. A bell would be a lie here — a handful of over-expressed genes drags the mean upward.

Sampling: why the mean keeps dancing

You never measure everyone. You take a sample and judge the whole by it. And here's where the treachery hides: the sample mean is itself a random quantity. Take a different ten people and you'll get a different mean.

🎬 Analogy

Think of shifts. One team took in three heart attacks over its shift; another, none at all. Does that mean the first team "attracts infarcts"? No — that's the spread between shifts. Judge a department's performance by a single shift and you'll fool yourself. A small sample shouts louder than it speaks. That's exactly why the mean of two small groups can dance back and forth even when there's no real difference at all.

Hence the golden rule of intuition: the smaller the group, the harder the mean dances, and the more cautiously you should believe any difference. Three mice per group is almost always noise pretending to be signal.

p-value: what it really is (and what it is NOT)

Now for the most slandered number in science. Let's settle it once and for all.

💡 The gist

The p-value answers exactly one question: "If there were actually NO difference, how often would pure chance draw me a difference this big or bigger?" A small p means "chance has a hard time explaining this." And that's it. It says nothing more.

What a p-value does NOT mean — here's the list worth memorizing:

  • It's not the probability that the hypothesis is true. p = 0.03 doesn't mean "the effect is 97% real."
  • It's not the size of the effect. A tiny, clinically meaningless difference in a huge sample easily gives p < 0.001.
  • It's not the probability that you're wrong. And p = 0.049 isn't "twice as bad" as 0.024 — the 0.05 line was drawn by a human, not by nature.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

A p-value is a lab flag "↑," not a diagnosis. The flag says "the value stepped outside the expected," but you don't treat the flag. The real question is how far outside, and whether that changes your management. It's exactly the same with statistics: significance is the flag, but the effect size makes the decision.

Type I and Type II errors, and power

Any "there's an effect / there's no effect" decision has two ways to blow it — and you already know both under different names.

  • Type I error (false positive). You shouted "there's a difference!" and there isn't. That's a false positive — overdiagnosis. A 0.05 threshold means you've agreed in advance to be wrong roughly 1 time in 20.
  • Type II error (false negative). There is a difference, and you missed it. That's a false negative — a missed diagnosis.
  • Power. The ability of a test to notice an effect when there really is one — the "keen-sightedness" of the study. A small sample is short-sighted: it sees a real difference only if it's enormous.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

This is just sensitivity and specificity, only applied to a study rather than to a test! A Type I error is like a false-positive screen: you scared a healthy person. A Type II error is like a tumor missed on an early scan. And power is the sensitivity of your experiment. A "negative result in three mice" isn't "there's no effect" — it's "the study was blind," exactly the way a negative test with low sensitivity doesn't rule out the diagnosis.

Effect size matters more than p

Here's the shift in thinking that separates the mature researcher from the novice. The novice asks "is it significant?" The mature one asks "how big is the difference, and does it matter?"

🎬 Analogy

A drug lowers blood pressure "with statistical significance, p < 0.001" — by a whole 1.5 mmHg. Significant? Yes. Does anyone need it? No. Another drug drops pressure by 15 mmHg but was tested on 12 patients, p = 0.08. Which would you rather study next? Effect size is clinical significance. The p-value only tells you whether the effect was a dream. A big effect with a shaky p is begging you to repeat it on a larger group. A negligible effect with a brilliant p is begging you to do nothing.

A study of 40,000 people: a new marker raises risk "significantly," p = 0.0004, but the absolute increase in risk is 0.1%. How should you read this?

In gigantic samples even a microscopic difference yields a tiny p. The p confirms the effect isn't chance, but the magnitude is set by the effect size — here 0.1%, which is clinically all but empty.

Confidence intervals: a range instead of a single number

A single number ("the risk is 1.8 times higher") lies through its precision. A confidence interval is more honest: it shows a range of plausible values. "Risk 1.8-fold, 95% CI from 1.1 to 2.9" — there's the whole truth at once: both the estimate and how shaky it is.

Reading one is a pleasure, once you know two tricks:

  • Width = how much you know. A narrow interval means lots of data and a firm estimate. A wide one means little data and you're all but guessing.
  • What's inside is what matters. For a risk ratio, "does the interval cross 1?" is exactly the same as "did the p cross the 0.05 threshold?" But the interval gives you more: it shows which effects you haven't yet ruled out.

Where does this range even come from, if the data are scant and there's no elegant formula? There's a stupidly honest trick — the bootstrap: resample your own data thousands of times (randomly drawing observations from it with replacement), recompute the answer each time, and watch it wander. The spread of those thousands of answers is your uncertainty, obtained without a single formula — literally "shake the data and see how much the result wobbles."

And one more thing that's easy to trip over: a confidence interval is a frequentist beast, and it says not quite what you'd like to hear ("in 95 of 100 identical experiments, the interval will cover the truth"). A credible interval (Bayesian), on the other hand, says exactly what your intuition was hoping for: "the very 95% chance that the parameter really lies here." The distinction is subtle, but honest intuition does well to keep it in mind: a frequentist CI is about the behavior of the procedure; a Bayesian credible interval is about the probability of the parameter itself.

💡 The gist

If you're offered a choice between "p = 0.04" and a confidence interval — always take the interval. p hides both the magnitude and the uncertainty. The interval shows both. A single number is a verdict; an interval is an honest conversation.

Multiple testing: the killer of omics

And now for that very trap from the start of the chapter, in full bloom. It's the single most common reason loud "discoveries" in genomics later fail to replicate.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already distrust one slightly deviant value in a broad panel. Order 20 tests for a healthy person and one is almost sure to pop up "out of range" purely by chance. You know this in your gut and don't chase such a flag. Well, RNA-seq isn't 20 tests — it's 20,000 at once.

The logic is merciless. At a 0.05 threshold you've agreed to 1 false alarm in 20. Test one gene — the risk is small. Test 20,000 genes where nothing is actually happening, and you get about a thousand "significant" results from pure noise. A thousand false discoveries, each with a proud p < 0.05. That's why a list of "differentially expressed genes" with no correction is garbage, prettily sorted.

The cure is FDR (false discovery rate), most often via the Benjamini–Hochberg method. The intuition is simple and elegant: instead of "let each test be wrong 1 time in 20," FDR says something different — "among the genes I ultimately call findings, let no more than, say, 5% be false." It raises the bar higher the more tests you ran. The number you read afterward is called a q-value — a p that has wised up to the scale.

In RNA-seq across 20,000 genes where there are no real differences at all, how many genes will give "p < 0.05" purely by chance?

5% of 20,000 is 1,000 false "findings" out of pure noise. That's exactly why gene lists without a multiplicity correction (FDR) don't replicate. The q-value saves you by controlling the fraction of false results among those declared significant.

PCA: how to compress a hundred readings into a couple of axes

Picture a patient with forty measured parameters. You don't actually hold forty independent numbers in your head — you collapse them into syndromes: "septic-looking," "volume-overloaded," "toxidrome." One word instead of forty.

PCA (principal component analysis) does precisely this, only formally. It looks at the data cloud in forty-dimensional space and finds a few directions along which the spread is greatest, discarding the boring, correlated repeats. Forty axes turn into two or three "principal" ones, along which you can finally draw a picture your eyes can read.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

This isn't an abstraction — it works literally. Run PCA on COPD patients, and the math, all on its own with no hints, sorts them into recognizable clinical phenotypes: "bronchitic," "emphysematous," "asthma-overlap." The algorithm rediscovered syndromes that clinicians spent centuries telling apart by eye. PCA is your habit of folding a constellation of features into one diagnosis, only expressed in numbers.

🔬 Deeper: what "principal components" actually are

Each new axis is a blend of the original features. The first component captures the biggest spread; the second captures the biggest of what's left, but always "at right angles" to the first (that is, about something else). This way you accumulate a handful of axes that together explain the lion's share of the differences between patients. Then you can plot the patient-points on the plane of the first two components — and see clusters with your own eyes. You won't have to compute any matrices: what matters is feeling that PCA looks for where the data diverge most interestingly, and looks precisely there.

Bayesian thinking = your clinical reasoning

Now for the most delightful part: you already have a built-in Bayesian engine, and you run it every single day.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

A positive troponin in a 25-year-old with heartburn and in a 70-year-old with crushing chest pain are two completely different positive tests. Why? Because you started from a different pre-test probability. The test result only shifts your confidence; it doesn't set it from scratch. That's Bayes' theorem in words: the pre-test probability is your "prior," the strength of the test is the "update," the post-test probability is the result. You don't work this out on paper — you feel it. "I wasn't worried until this result came back — and now I am" — that's Bayes in a nutshell.

The practical takeaway, which transfers everywhere: data update a belief, they don't create one out of thin air. A weak signal against a strong prior of "this can't be" is a reason to recheck the instrument, not to rewrite the textbook. An extraordinary claim demands extraordinary data precisely because it starts from a tiny prior.

Four traps that spoil even honest data

Data can be flawlessly collected — and still lie. Four classic snares.

  • Correlation ≠ causation. Two phenomena move in lockstep — that doesn't mean one causes the other. Often both are pulled by a third, hidden factor (a confounder). Ice-cream sales "predict" drownings — because summer dictates both, not the ice cream.
  • Batch effects. The patients' samples were processed on Monday, the healthy controls' on Thursday, with a different reagent lot. The instrument found a "powerful difference between groups" — except it's a difference between days, not between disease and health. A technical artifact wearing a mask of biology. The cure is to mix the groups across batches, not split them by batch.
  • Biological vs. technical replicates. Three samples from one mouse tell you how stable your instrument is. Three samples from three different mice tell you about biology. They're not interchangeable: technical replicates can't be passed off as evidence that "it's like this in all mice" — otherwise you're confidently generalizing the fate of one mouse to the whole species.
  • Data leakage. The model accidentally peeked at the answer during training — for instance, a feature crept into the data that is actually a consequence of the diagnosis rather than its cause. On the test set it shines; in the real clinic it flops. It's like a student who "aced" the exam because the questions were leaked in advance.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

Data leakage and batch effects are twins of your familiar pain: "the score worked beautifully at the hospital where it was invented, and flopped at ours." The model memorized the quirks of its own sample — its noise, its instruments, its Mondays — not the disease itself. That's why your reflex "I don't trust a rule until it's tested on patients like mine" is exactly a demand for external validation, the healthiest instinct in applied statistics.

The patients were processed on one day, the healthy controls on another, with different reagents. The instrument showed a "huge difference between groups." Your first suspicion?

When "group" lines up perfectly with "day of processing," you can't tell biology from technique. This is a classic batch effect — a technical artifact masquerading as a result. The cure is to mix the groups across batches.

🎯 Key takeaways

  • Variation is always there. The question isn't "are the numbers different," but "are they different by more than the usual spread." A small sample shouts louder than it speaks.
  • A p-value is a flag, not a diagnosis. It only answers "is this hard to explain by chance." It's not the probability of being right, and not the effect size. Effect size and the confidence interval matter more.
  • Multiple testing is the killer of omics. 20,000 tests on noise will yield ~1,000 false "findings." Always correct for multiplicity (FDR / q-value), or your gene list is prettily sorted garbage.
  • PCA folds dozens of features into a couple of axes — exactly the way you fold a constellation of symptoms into a single syndrome; on COPD it rediscovers clinical phenotypes on its own.
  • Bayes = your clinical reasoning. Data update a belief; they don't create it. The pre-test probability decides how to read the result.
  • Even honest data lie: correlation ≠ causation, batch effects, confusing biological and technical replicates, data leakage. Your reflex "test it on my patients" is a demand for external validation — and it's golden.
💻

Chapter 4

Computational thinking and Python (without fear)

⏱ ~20 min · After this chapter you'll read someone else's code like a case history and understand what's happening inside — no panic, no memorizing syntax

Imagine someone hands you a spreadsheet of 40,000 patients and asks you to find everyone whose potassium is above 6.0 mmol/L. With a mouse in Excel, that's an evening of your life gone. In one line of Python, it's half a second. The question isn't whether the computer is smart. The question is who asks it — in words it understands.

Why would a doctor need code if there are programmers

The short answer: because a programmer doesn't know that a potassium of 6.1 with a widened QRS is no longer just an "elevated value" — it's a call to the ICU. You know that. Data without clinical meaning is just numbers, and the meaning lives in your head.

Code isn't about becoming an engineer. It's about refusing to be a hostage to someone else's schedule. Every time you ask someone to "pull me this subgroup" and wait three days, you lose the momentum of a thought. A scientific idea lives for hours, not weeks. Code gives you back your speed.

And one more thing, less obvious. When you describe an analysis in code, you describe it precisely. "I excluded the outliers" is not a method. "I removed values above the 99th percentile with this line" is a method — one anyone can reproduce. Code is the language science is obliged to speak in if it wants to be believed.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already think algorithmically every single day. The ACLS protocol is code: "If the rhythm is shockable → defibrillate → 2 minutes of CPR → check the rhythm → repeat." Conditions, loops, branches. Programming is that same protocol, just written down so that it's carried out not by an exhausted intern at 4 a.m., but by a machine that doesn't make mistakes when it's tired.

A mental model of Python: six building blocks

Forget about "learning the language." Python rests on a handful of ideas, and you already have all of them — just under different names. Let's take them not as a course, but as a way of thinking.

A variable is a labeled test tube. You put a value inside and stick a label on it so you can find it later.

potassium = 6.1

Now, everywhere you write potassium, the machine will substitute 6.1. You can peel the label off and stick it on a different tube at any moment — the language doesn't forbid it.

A type is what's in the tube, and it decides what you're allowed to do with it. Numbers get added, text gets joined together, but adding text to a number is something the machine will refuse to do — just as you'd refuse to mix incompatible drugs.

  • int — whole numbers: patient counts, age in years.
  • float — decimals: potassium, temperature, dose in mg/kg.
  • str — text: diagnosis, name, sex.
  • bool — yes/no: whether there's an allergy or not.

💡 The gist

A variable is a name for a value. A type is the nature of the value, which decides what you may lawfully do with it. Everything else in Python is ways of packing values into structures and running them through actions.

A list is an ordered queue. Patients in order of admission, glucose readings hour by hour. There's an order, and access is by position number.

potassiums = [4.2, 5.8, 6.1, 3.9]

The first element is potassiums[0] (yes, counting from zero is the first thing that annoys everyone, and the first thing you get used to).

A dictionary is a patient chart. Not "slot #3," but "field: value." You ask not by number, but by the name of the field.

patient = {"age": 67, "diagnosis": "CAD", "potassium": 6.1}

And you retrieve what you need: patient["potassium"] returns 6.1. This is exactly the logic you use to read the header of a case history: not "the third line from the top," but "the Diagnosis field."

🩺 Bridge from medicine

List versus dictionary is the difference between a queue at the front desk and an alphabetical card file. In the queue, you find a person by position ("the third one"). In the card file, by name ("Ivanov"). Use a list when order matters. Use a dictionary when the name of the field matters. A medical record is a dictionary nested inside a dictionary: patient → labs → potassium.

A conditional (if) is a branch — your daily bread. "If potassium is above 6.0, sound the alarm."

if patient["potassium"] > 6.0:
    print("Hyperkalemia — check the ECG")

Notice the indentation. In Python, indentation isn't decoration, it's grammar. Shifting to the right says: "this belongs to the condition above." Like a paragraph in a case history that belongs to its own section.

A loop means "do this to each one." Don't write the command 40,000 times — say "for each patient."

for patient in ward:
    check_potassium(patient)

This is your morning rounds, written in a single phrase. "For each one in the room, assess their condition." The machine won't get tired by the fortieth bed.

A function is a protocol you describe once and then call by name afterward. You don't recite the intubation algorithm every time — you say "let's intubate" and everyone understands the whole process.

def acetaminophen_dose(weight_kg):
    return weight_kg * 15

Now acetaminophen_dose(20) returns 300. You described the rule "15 mg per kg" once — and it works for any weight, without slip-ups in mental arithmetic at a child's bedside.

For each patient you're storing a set of named fields: age, diagnosis, a list of labs. Which data structure fits best?

A dictionary stores "key: value" pairs, like the fields on a medical record. You reach for the data by meaning ("diagnosis"), not by slot number — which is exactly what a patient chart calls for.

🔬 Deeper: why counting starts at zero

An index is really an "offset from the start." The first element sits right at the beginning, offset zero. The second is one position further along, offset one. This is a legacy of how memory is physically laid out: the address of an element = the address of the start + position × size. Starting from zero makes the arithmetic cleaner. You don't need to memorize this — just accept that [0] means "the first one," and your brain will rewire within a week.

pandas: Excel on steroids

Everything above is the foundation. But the real work with data you'll do through a tool called pandas. Picture Excel with the mouse taken away and a language handed over instead. Rather than "select, filter, copy," you describe what you want — and get the result instantly, across millions of rows, reproducibly.

The central hero is the DataFrame. It's a table: rows are observations (patients, samples, genes), columns are features (age, potassium, expression). The same Excel sheet, but living inside code.

Want everyone with hyperkalemia? You don't scroll through 40,000 rows — you pose a condition to the whole table at once:

severe = df[df["potassium"] > 6.0]

It reads almost like English: "take the rows from the table where potassium is greater than 6." In a fraction of a second, severe contains only the subgroup you need. It's your perfect search query over the registry, only without waiting on the IT department.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

A DataFrame is your summary table for the cohort, and filtering is the inclusion and exclusion criteria of your study design. When you write df[df["age"] >= 18], you're literally stating the inclusion criterion "adults only." Every line of code is one line in the Methods section — only executable.

Vectorization: why a loop is slow

Here's a subtle but important point. You could compute the mean potassium by looping over every patient and adding as you go. It would work. But on large data, that's like visiting each patient one at a time, in person. There's another way — vectorization: telling it "compute the mean over the entire column" in a single command.

df["potassium"].mean()

Why is this dozens of times faster? Because behind the scenes pandas hands the whole column at once to a powerful, optimized engine that processes numbers in batches, rather than one by one through slow Python. A loop asks "this one? this one? this one?" forty thousand times. A vector operation says "here are all forty thousand, give me the answer."

🎬 Analogy

A loop is the charge nurse personally taking the blood pressure of each of forty patients, one after another, writing each into her notebook. Vectorization is when all forty beds have monitors wired up, and one glance at the station shows you every blood pressure at once. Same result. The difference is whether you process the stream one after another or all at once. The rule is simple: if you feel the urge to write a loop over the rows of a table, first look for a ready-made operation over the whole column. There almost always is one.

Why is df["potassium"].mean() faster than a loop that adds potassium patient by patient?

It's about how the computation is done: a vector operation passes the whole array to a low-level engine that processes the numbers in parallel, bypassing the step-by-step Python loop. Same result — different speed.

The pipeline: analysis as an assembly line

Real analysis isn't one command — it's a pipeline: a chain of steps where the output of each becomes the input of the next. Load the raw data → clean it → filter the cohort → compute → plot. Each step does one clear job.

The beauty is that a pipeline is written down and repeatable. You don't have to remember "what did I click last Tuesday." The whole path from raw material to result lies before you as lines you can rerun, tweak one step, and get an updated result.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

A pipeline is the pre-analytical and analytical stages of the lab. Draw the blood → spin it down → dilute it → run it through the analyzer → get the result. If there's an error at some step (hemolysis!), the result is unreliable, and you know exactly which stage to look at. A code pipeline gives you that same traceability: you can see where the data might have gone bad.

Reproducible environments: so that "works on my machine" isn't an excuse

Your analysis uses pandas of such-and-such version, plus five other libraries at specific versions. On your laptop, everything works. You send the code to a colleague — and it all falls over, because they have a different version. That familiar horror of incompatibility.

The cure is a virtual environment (venv / conda). It's a separate "clean room" for a specific project, holding exactly the versions it needs and nothing extra. Projects don't interfere with each other, and the list of dependencies can be shipped along with the code.

🎬 Analogy

A virtual environment is a sterile, dedicated surgical set for each operation. You don't perform an appendectomy with instruments that "were lying around somewhere after the last shift." Each project gets its own sealed tray with precisely known contents. Then the result is reproducible, and "works on my machine" turns into "works on everyone's, because the set is one and the same."

Git: a time machine for your work

You've been editing an analysis, broke something, and now you want to go back to the version "the way it was on Wednesday, when everything computed correctly." Without a tool, that's impossible — it's not as if you were saving analysis_final_final_this_one_for_sure.py. With Git, it's a single command.

Git photographs the state of your project at moments you choose (these are called commits). Each commit is a snapshot of "here's how everything looked right now, and here's what I changed." The entire history is preserved. You can return to any snapshot, compare two versions, and see exactly what changed between Tuesday and Wednesday.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

Git is the progress notes in a case history, only for code. Each dated entry records the state at a point in time and what changed: "added a diuretic, urine output rose." You can always page back and see when and why you made a decision. And, as in a good case history, the value isn't in the entries themselves, but in being able to trace the flow of thought and change over time.

💡 The gist

Three tools protect your science from chaos: a virtual environment pins down what you computed with, a pipeline pins down how you computed, and Git pins down when and what changed. Together they turn an analysis from "magic on my laptop" into a reproducible result.

The command line and servers: you already know how to do this

You've already connected over ssh to a remote machine — which means the scary phrase "command line" isn't foreign to you. The command line (terminal) is simply a conversation with the computer in text instead of clicks. One line often replaces a dozen mouse movements and, more importantly, it can be recorded and repeated.

Why remote servers and HPC clusters? Because a genomic analysis or training a model can demand more memory and hours than your laptop will ever give. You connect over ssh to a machine with hundreds of cores, launch the computation there, close your laptop lid, and go to sleep. In the morning, the result is ready.

🩺 Bridge from medicine
