The heart of the method is the hypothesis: a testable statement that could turn out to be false. "This drug lowers mortality" is a hypothesis. "Medicine matters" is not, because you can't disprove it. A good hypothesis is sharp: it tells you in advance which result would kill it.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

A hypothesis is your working diagnosis, and the experiment is the test you order to confirm or discard it. An experienced physician orders the test that will change the picture the most: if the D-dimer is negative, PE is all but ruled out. A good hypothesis works the same way — it predicts a result capable of disproving it. A statement that "explains" any outcome is diagnostically useless.

Hiding here is one of the most important distinctions in science — exploratory versus confirmatory research. These are two completely different modes, and confusing them is the source of half our troubles.

Exploration is when you wander through the data with no predefined hypothesis: you plot dozens of graphs, hunt for patterns, generate ideas. That's wonderful and necessary. But exploratory findings are suspicions, not conclusions. Confirmation is when you state one specific hypothesis in advance, collect fresh data, and test that exact hypothesis, tweaking nothing along the way.

💡 The gist

Exploration generates hypotheses. Confirmation tests them. The cardinal sin is to run an exploration, stumble onto a beautiful pattern, and present it as if you'd been looking for that exact thing all along. It's like doing a whole-body ultrasound, finding an incidental cyst, and declaring that you "predicted" it would show up.

You ran 40 blood markers in patients who recovered and patients who fell ill, and noticed that zinc levels were "almost significantly" different. What is this?

You didn't state a hypothesis about zinc in advance — you stumbled onto it after sifting through 40 variables. This is classic exploration: a valuable lead, but you have to test it on fresh, independent data.

Experimental design: think before, not after

Half the reproducibility traps are closed not by clever statistics at the end, but by what you thought through in advance — before the first test tube.

💡 The gist

Biological vs technical replicates. Running the same sample twice on an instrument is a technical replicate: it measures the noise of the instrument, not biology. Your real n is set by biological replicates — different patients, different animals, different independent cultures. Three blood draws from one person is n=1, not n=3.

Power and sample size — calculate them at the planning stage. Estimate in advance: if the effect is real and of a given size, how many observations do you need to see it? Too small a sample will "miss" even a genuine effect (and you'll have wasted patients and reagents for nothing); the smaller the n, the "jumpier" and less reliable the result. It's the same intuition as with test sensitivity: a weak signal needs a big enough sample to be caught.

Randomizing across batches — your defense against batch effects. If you process all your "sick" samples on Monday and all your "healthy" ones on Thursday, then the day, the reagent, and the instrument's mood are welded to the group forever, and you can never separate them afterward. Mix samples from both groups across batches (plates, days, reagent lots) in advance — then the technical noise smears itself across the groups instead of masquerading as a "discovery."

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already know this from the clinic. Three back-to-back blood-pressure readings on the same arm are not three patients but one; to say anything about "people in general," you need different people. And the habit of running the control and the test in the same run, on the same calibrator, is the same randomization against a "reagent-lot effect." A study's design is thought through before enrollment, exactly like a management protocol before the patient is admitted.

You drew blood from one patient and ran three parallel measurements on the analyzer. What is your biological n?

The number of independent biological units is one person, so n=1. Repeated measurements of one sample assess the instrument's precision (technical replicates), but add no independent observations. You can't generalize to "patients" from them.

How to read a paper: pictures first, criticism second

A scientific paper is built on the IMRAD skeleton: Introduction (why), Methods (how), Results (what came out), Discussion (what it means). The A in the acronym is just the connecting "and" between Results and Discussion. Plus the Abstract — a compressed version of the whole thing. A beginner reads a paper straight through, from the first word. An expert doesn't.

The professional reading order is this: Abstract → all figures and tables → Methods → and only then the text. Why? Because the figures are the results, without the authors' rhetoric. Data can't talk you into anything; text can. First look at what was actually shown, form your own opinion — and only then compare it against how the authors interpret it.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You already do this on clinical rounds. Someone presents a patient, and you don't swallow your colleague's conclusion whole — you look at the actual scans, the actual numbers, the actual monitor traces, and you ask, "Was the sampling technique right? Was there a control?" A critical read of a paper is the same round, only the patient is made of paper. The question "do I believe these methods?" is native to you.

To critique a paper is to attack its weakest link: the methods and the statistics. The key questions: Was there a control group? Is the sample large enough (n)? Was the "significance" threshold chosen after the fact? Does what the authors promised to study match what they ultimately showed?

🩺 Bridge from medicine

This is exactly the logic of evidence-based medicine. You no longer prescribe a drug just because "it worked in one paper": you ask about the design, the randomization, the blinding, the effect size, and the confidence interval. The hierarchy of evidence — from expert opinion up to a meta-analysis of RCTs — is formalized skepticism toward other people's methods. Reading a paper is no different.

🔬 Deeper: the confidence interval matters more than the p-value

A p-value only tells you "how surprising the result would be if there were no effect." It doesn't tell you whether the effect is large. A confidence interval shows the range of plausible values for the effect — and that's far more useful. A drug can "statistically significantly" lower mortality by 0.3% — significant, but clinically meaningless. You've already been trained in this in medicine: the difference between "statistically significant" and "does this change what I prescribe" is the difference between a p-value and an effect size.

The reproducibility crisis: how honest people fool themselves

Now the most important part — and the most insidious. Science is going through a reproducibility crisis: a huge fraction of published results don't hold up on re-testing. And most often it isn't fraud. It's a set of traps that the mind falls into on its own, with the best of intentions.

P-hacking is tweaking the analysis until the result "becomes significant." Drop a couple of "inconvenient" patients, try a different statistical test, split the data into subgroups — and there, p has fallen below the coveted threshold. Each step seems innocent. The sum is self-deception.

🎬 Analogy

Picture a shooter who first blasts away at the side of a barn, then paints a target around the densest cluster of holes. From a distance — a sniper. In reality — cheating on the order of operations. This is HARKing (Hypothesizing After the Results are Known) — inventing a hypothesis after the results are already in. The target is painted around the hits. Science demands the reverse: first the target (hypothesis), then the shot (data).

Even trickier is the garden of forking paths. Even if you honestly didn't run 20 different analyses, at every step you made decisions: how to define a "severe case," whom to exclude, where to draw the age cutoff. Had the data been different, you'd have made different decisions — reasonable ones too. A multitude of "reasonable paths" through the data lead to different conclusions, and you walked down the one that gave a pretty result, without even noticing the forks.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You know this trap under a different name: "order 20 tests on a healthy person and one will come back falsely abnormal purely by chance." Multiple comparisons are the same problem. That's exactly why you instinctively distrust a single isolated deviation on a wide panel. P-hacking is when a researcher orders those very 20 tests and then reports only the one that "fired."

And the cherry on top — publication bias. Journals love "positive," splashy results. Studies that "found nothing" often don't get published at all — they gather dust in the file drawer. As a result, the literature is systematically skewed: you see the lucky coin flips and don't see the unlucky ones. A drug that "worked" in one out of twenty trials looks effective if the other nineteen never made it to print.

A researcher got unremarkable data, then split the patients into subgroups by sex, age, and stage — and in one subgroup found p<0.05. What do you call this?

Sifting through subgroups until a "significant" result appears is multiple comparisons without correction. The more slices you make, the likelier a chance "success." An honest subanalysis is declared in advance and treated as exploratory.

Scientific writing: honesty built into the structure

When you write your own paper, that same IMRAD works as an honesty checklist. The Introduction poses the question. The Methods describe everything in enough detail that another person could repeat it — that's the very definition of reproducibility. The Results show the data without embellishment. The Discussion honestly names the limitations.

A genre of its own is the proposal (or grant): you justify why the study is worth the money and time, before it's even been carried out. Here the hypothesis and analysis plan are locked in advance — which, as we'll see, protects against p-hacking all by itself.

💡 The gist

Honest visualization isn't decoration — it's ethics. A truncated Y-axis that turns a 2% difference into a "mountain"; a chart where the thickness of the lines shouts louder than the data; a dropped "inconvenient" point — all of this is lying in the language of pictures. The rule is simple: a figure should help the reader see the truth in the data, not your hope for it.

And the red line you must never cross is plagiarism: passing off someone else's words, ideas, or data as your own. In science this isn't "strictness for its own sake": the whole system rests on knowing exactly who did what and whose shoulders they stood on. Cite generously. Someone else's idea, honestly credited to its author, adorns your work; a stolen one destroys it.

Presenting and pitching: to the point in 60 seconds

Data that no one understood is like a diagnosis you never told the patient: useless. A good presentation isn't "show everything I did" — it's "walk the listener from the problem to the finding down one clear road."

The same arc works as in a good case history at rounds: problem → why it matters → what you did → what you found → what it changes. One slide, one idea. If the audience remembers one sentence — which sentence should it be? Start with that. A pitch is the same arc, squeezed down to a minute for someone who has no time: hook, gist, significance.

🎬 Analogy

Think of a talk as handing off a critically ill patient to the on-call shift. You have 30 seconds, and your colleague has to walk away understanding the essentials and knowing what to do. You don't read out the entire case history from birth — you give the gist, the context, and the next step. A bad talk is when the listener nods and then can't retell what the work was even about.

Research ethics: the patient matters more than the discovery

Here you're more at home than any engineer. The three pillars of research ethics are familiar to you to the point of reflex.

Informed consent: a person takes part only after understanding what they're getting into, and can withdraw at any moment. Privacy: data is de-identified, protected, and doesn't surface where it shouldn't. Research integrity: don't make up data (fabrication), don't doctor it (falsification), don't steal it (plagiarism) — the "unholy trinity" of scientific misconduct.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

Informed consent in a study is the same document and the same ethics as what you sign before a procedure. The principle is identical: patient autonomy trumps scientific curiosity. "It'll help science" is no excuse for bypassing consent, exactly as "it's an interesting case" doesn't justify an intervention without permission. The oath you've already taken covers the research bench too.

💡 The gist

When working with data, privacy isn't a box you tick for show. De-identified data can sometimes be re-identified by cross-referencing a few "harmless" fields (postal code, date of birth, a rare diagnosis). That's why data protection is an active engineering task, not a one-time "we deleted the names."

Intellectual property: from idea to product

Suppose your research gave birth to something useful — a new molecule, an algorithm, a device. How do you turn it into something that reaches patients? This is where intellectual property (IP) comes in.

A patent is a temporary state-granted monopoly on your invention in exchange for publicly disclosing it. The logic is a bargain: "tell everyone how it works, and for 20 years we'll protect you from copying." The key, counterintuitive point: publishing before you file often kills the patent — a disclosed idea is no longer "new." That's why in startups the order is sometimes the reverse of the academic one: patent first, paper second.

🎬 Analogy

A patent is like a protected prescription pad: you disclose the exact formula, but only you are allowed to "write" it for the agreed term. A license is then when you allow a pharmacy (a company) to compound your drug for a share of the revenue (a royalty). And a startup is when you open the pharmacy yourself to get the drug onto the shelf. Three different paths from idea to patient: protect it, let others do it, build it yourself.

Not every discovery needs to be patented, and not everything can be. But understanding the difference between "publish," "patent," and "license" is critical: one careless conference talk can rob a discovery of its commercial future.

You've invented a diagnostic algorithm with genuine commercial potential. What's wiser to do first?

Public disclosure (a paper or a talk) usually destroys the novelty a patent requires. If there's commercial potential, you file the application first and only then reveal it to the world.

Open science: the antidote to the crisis

Remember all those reproducibility traps? They have a common antidote — open science: making the process transparent, not just the final conclusion.

Code goes on GitHub, so anyone can see exactly what you computed. Data and materials go on Zenodo with a permanent link. Genomic data goes into dedicated banks like GEO / SRA, so others can re-check your analysis on your own raw data.

💡 The gist

The most powerful defense against p-hacking and the "garden of forking paths" is preregistration: you publicly lock in your hypothesis and analysis plan before collecting data. After that, you can't paint the target around the hits — the target is fixed and dated. Openness turns honesty from a private virtue into a verifiable fact.

Open science isn't about altruism. Transparent work gets reproduced, gets cited, and outlives its authors. Closed work risks turning out to be that very "false finding" from Ioannidis's paper.

🎯 Key takeaways

  • You already think like a scientist: a hypothesis is a working diagnosis, an experiment is the decisive test. A good hypothesis is sharp and falsifiable.
  • Exploration generates hypotheses, confirmation tests them — on fresh data. Passing off an exploratory finding as a confirmed one is the root of many false results.
  • Design is thought through before enrollment: your n is set by biological replicates, sample size is estimated in advance, and samples are randomized across batches — otherwise technical noise will masquerade as a discovery.
  • Read papers like a clinical case review: figures and methods first, then the text; attack the weakest link — the design and statistics. This is your evidence-based medicine.
  • The reproducibility crisis is born not of malice but of p-hacking, HARKing, the "garden of forking paths," and publication bias. Multiple comparisons you already know as "20 tests on a healthy person."
  • Research ethics is your turf: informed consent, privacy, integrity. Patient autonomy trumps curiosity.
  • IP decides a discovery's fate: disclosure before the patent kills the novelty. Open science and preregistration turn honesty from a virtue into a verifiable fact.
🎓

Epilogue

Your path to 80%: how it all connects and what comes next

⏱ ~10 min · After this chapter you'll have a map: what to learn first, how to know you've truly nailed a topic, and where to go next — with medicine as your trump card, not your handicap.

You made it to the end. Behind you: statistics, programming, omics, a little chemistry, and machine learning. But in your head it's probably all sitting in separate boxes. The book's final task is to fuse those boxes into one living organism. Because in a real project they never work alone.

One question — the whole book at once

Let's follow a single story from start to finish. Not an invented one — this is how nearly every modern biomedical project works. Imagine a clinical question lands on your desk, the doctor's: "Why does COPD run aggressively in some patients and smoulder in others, when their spirometry looks the same?"

Watch how the whole book gets pulled along that thread — one discipline at a time, like consultants on a grand round.

  • The clinic asks the question. This is your turf. You frame a hypothesis: maybe behind identical spirometry hide different biological subtypes of the disease.
  • Data and statistics bring order. You assemble a cohort, clean the table, look at the distributions. Everything you know about samples, bias, and confidence intervals comes into play here.
  • Omics opens the hood. You take samples and run RNA-seq — reading the expression of thousands of genes. Differential expression reveals which genes are "on fire" in the aggressive group.
  • Dimensionality reduction draws the map. You compress forty correlated features with PCA into a couple of axes — and suddenly clusters emerge on the plot (a detailed walk-through of PCA on COPD is in the statistics chapter).
  • Machine learning builds the model. You train a classifier to predict the subtype from available markers, check it with cross-validation, and — always — on an external cohort.
  • And the clinic closes the loop. A model is useless if it doesn't change a decision at the bedside. You circle back to the question: does this actually help you tailor the therapy?

💡 The gist

The disciplines in this book aren't a list of subjects — they're a pipeline. Clinical question → data → statistics → omics → dimensionality reduction → model → back to the clinic. Every chapter was one station on that pipeline. Now you can see the whole factory floor.

🎬 Analogy

It's like a big case conference. The pulmonologist poses the question, the lab tech brings the results, the geneticist reads the expression, the statistician says "you can trust this result, but not that one," and in the end everyone comes back to the patient. No single participant makes the diagnosis alone. Data science is you, having learned to invite math and bioinformatics to the conference too.

Your personal sequence: what to learn first

The temptation is to learn everything at once, all in parallel. Don't. There's an order that works best for a doctor, because each next layer rests on the one before it instead of hanging in mid-air.

  • Step 1. Thinking in data. Not code, not formulas — the habit of looking at a table and asking: where do these numbers come from, who's missing from them, where's the bias. You already have this — it's how you read a patient history.
  • Step 2. Basic Python as a screwdriver. Not "become a programmer," but being able to load a table, filter it, plot a graph. Three or four tools you'll use every day.
  • Step 3. Applied statistics. Not deriving formulas, but what a p-value, a confidence interval, an effect size actually mean — and why "statistically significant" ≠ "matters for the patient."
  • Step 4. Visualization and exploratory analysis (EDA). The knack of looking at your data first, before you start computing. A good chart saves you a month of false hypotheses.
  • Step 5. Your domain — omics or imaging. This is where you choose where to dive deeper: genomics, proteomics, medical imaging. It's like picking a specialty.
  • Step 6. Machine learning — dead last. It's the trendiest and the most overrated by beginners. Without the first five steps, a model is just a beautiful lie built on dirty data.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You didn't start your training with neurosurgery. First anatomy, physiology, propaedeutics — and only then the narrow technique. It's the same here: ML without statistics is surgery without knowing anatomy. Gorgeous in theory, a catastrophe in practice.

A beginner colleague wants to "just train a neural network" on medical data, skipping statistics and exploratory analysis. What do you tell them?

A model is only as good as the data are clean and understood. Skip EDA and statistics, and it's easy to end up with a flashy model that actually learned the artifacts of data collection, not the disease.

The "I know it at 80%" checklist

Eighty percent isn't a random figure. It's the threshold past which you can work: understand someone else's analysis, hold a conversation with a bioinformatician as an equal, do an honest project yourself. The remaining 20% you pick up over years, as the need arises. Here's an honest checklist — not "read the chapter," but "can explain it to a friend and apply it."

  • Data and statistics: you can explain what sampling bias is, how correlation differs from causation, what a p-value means, and why with 20 tests one will "pop" by chance.
  • Programming: you can load a dataset yourself, clean it, plot a couple of graphs, and you don't panic at the sight of an error message — you read it.
  • Omics: you understand that RNA-seq is a "complete blood count for the whole genome," what fold-change is, and why you need a correction for multiple testing.
  • Dimensionality reduction and clustering: you can explain why you'd squeeze 40 features into 2 axes, and how supervised learning differs from unsupervised.
  • Machine learning: you know what overfitting is, why you need validation on external data, and why regularization is "Occam's razor" for a model.
  • Chemistry and structure: you see the "structure–function" link of a protein as an extension of familiar receptor pharmacology: affinity, lock and key, a point mutation.

💡 The gist

The 80% criterion is simple and strict: you can explain a concept to a colleague in one sentence without jargon — and you know where it breaks. If yes — that box is closed. If it's "I sort of get it, but I can't explain it" — it's still open.

🔬 Deeper: why "explain," not "read"

There's the Feynman technique: you've truly understood a topic only when you can lay it out in plain words for someone who isn't in the field. As long as your explanation has gaps you paper over with clever words, that's exactly where the misunderstanding is sitting. That's why the checklist is built on "explain it and show where it breaks," not on "tick the box that you read it." Passive recognition is deceptive: the text feels familiar, but you can't reproduce it. Active recall — that's what leaves a trace.

How to keep learning

The book is over — the learning isn't. But from here everything changes: now you learn by problems, not by chapters. And that's far more pleasant.

  • Practice on your own data. Not the textbook iris dataset, but something you genuinely care about: your specialty, your patients (anonymized!), your clinical question. Motivation from curiosity lasts many times longer than "I have to."
  • Treat topics like rotations. Just as in residency you try cardiology, then neurology, and figure out where you belong — so spend a month on genomics, a month on medical imaging, a month on ML. You choose a specialty with your hands, not from a brochure.
  • Learn what you need right now. Not "all of Python," but the exact tool that solves your current problem. Knowledge applied today sticks. Knowledge "for later" evaporates.
  • Come back to the basics in a spiral. Reread the statistics chapter in six months — you'll see twice as much in it, because now you have context. That's not treading water, it's rereading from a new depth.

🩺 Bridge from medicine

You're already a master of learning from real cases. No doctor ever learned from a textbook — you learn at the bedside, on case reviews, from your own mistakes. Data science works the same way: real skill comes not from reading but from wrestling with real, dirty, stubborn data. One project you carried through to the end is worth ten courses you completed.

You've closed the book. What's the most effective way to keep going?

Knowledge sets by applying it, not by hoarding it. One completed project on your own data will teach you more than a stack of half-read textbooks — and it will expose exactly the gaps worth closing.

The one exception: math you have to finish separately

All through this book I kept a promise: no deriving formulas, no "solve this integral." We took the intuition, not the algebra. And that was right — so you wouldn't drown on page one.

But be honest with yourself: that was a detour, not a cancellation. Math is the native language of this field. As long as you read it with a dictionary, you depend on someone else's translation. To stop depending at some point, you'll have to fill it in — separately and calmly, without this book's hurry.

  • A little linear algebra — because data live in it: a table is a matrix, PCA is its geometry.
  • The basics of probability — because all of statistics and all of ML stand on it, and without it they stay incantations.
  • The idea of a derivative — not the mechanics of computing, but the meaning: "how fast one thing changes in step with another." You already know this under the name "half-life."

🩺 Bridge from medicine

A differential equation sounds scary until you call it by your name for it: pharmacokinetics. The plasma concentration curve of a drug is the solution to the equation "how much went in, how much went out." You intuitively solve equations like this every time you think about a loading dose and a maintenance dose. Math doesn't give you a new ability — it gives a name to one you already have.

💡 The gist

Math is the only thing this book deliberately deferred, not dropped. Don't fear it and don't worship it. Fill it in later, in small portions, tying every concept to something you already do as a doctor.

Your weakness was your strength all along

You probably started this book with a quiet thought: "I'm a doctor, not a programmer — this'll be harder for me than for anyone." Time to flip that thought over.

An engineer coming into biomedicine spends years learning what's in your bones: how a disease works, what a number means for a living patient, what question is even worth asking. They can build models — but often build them about the wrong thing. You know what it's about. And that can't be googled.

🎬 Analogy

Picture two people at one microscope. One dials in the optics brilliantly but can't tell whether the tissue in front of them is healthy or diseased. The other spots the pathology at a glance but still fumbles with the knobs. Which one becomes whole faster? The one who already sees the pathology. Dialing in the optics is a matter of practice. Seeing the disease is years — years you already have.

💡 The gist

Data science gives you the tools. Medicine gives you the questions that are worth asking and the instinct for whether an answer rings true. Tools you can finish learning. Clinical thinking you can't. You're standing not at the back of the line, but in its rare part: at the intersection, where people are few and badly needed.

So 80% isn't the finish line. It's the threshold past which you're already useful: you can understand someone else's analysis, argue on the merits, do your own honest project. The rest you'll pick up along the way — on your own data, your own questions, at your own pace. Slow is fine. What matters is that you don't stop.

What's the main advantage a doctor entering data science has over an engineer?

Tools and math you can finish learning through practice. But clinical instinct — which question is meaningful and whether the result is plausible — is years at the bedside that can't be googled.

🎯 Key takeaways

  • The disciplines are a pipeline, not a list. From the clinical question through data, omics, and dimensionality reduction to a model — and back to the patient.
  • There's a right order. Thinking in data → Python → statistics → visualization → your own domain → ML dead last.
  • 80% = you can explain it and apply it. A threshold not for the box-tick, but for real work and a conversation among equals.
  • Keep learning on your own data. One completed project and "rotations" across topics beat a stack of courses.
  • Fill in the math separately. The one thing the book deferred rather than dropped — in small portions, through familiar physiology.
  • Medicine is your trump card. You know what question is worth asking. That's rare, and courses don't teach it.
⚡ 0 XP
